=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a simple undirected graph with N vertices and M edges, with vertices labeled 1 to N and edges labeled 1 to M. The i-th edge connects vertices U_i and V_i. Initially, G does not contain an odd cycle.
Takahashi and Aoki will play a game using this graph G. With Aoki going first, they take turns performing the following operation:

- Choose a pair of integers (i,j) with 1 \leq i < j \leq N that satisfies both of the following conditions, then add an edge connecting vertices i and j to G.
- G does not already have an edge connecting vertices i and j.
- Adding an edge connecting vertices i and j does not create an odd cycle.



A player who cannot perform this operation loses, and the other player wins.
Determine who wins when both players play optimally.

What is an odd cycle?
A sequence of vertices (v_0,v_1,\ldots,v_k) of G is called an odd cycle if and only if all of the following conditions are satisfied:

- k is odd.
- v_0=v_k.
- For every 1\leq i \leq k, there is an edge connecting v_{i-1} and v_{i}.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N M
U_1 V_1
U_2 V_2
\vdots
U_M V_M

Output

If Aoki (the first player) wins, print Aoki; otherwise, if Takahashi (the second player) wins, print Takahashi.

Constraints


- 1 \leq N \leq 2\times 10^5
- 0 \leq M \leq 2\times 10^5
- 1 \leq U_i < V_i \leq N
- The given graph does not contain an odd cycle.
- The given graph does not contain multi-edges.
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

4 3
1 2
2 3
3 4

Sample Output 1

Aoki

If Aoki (the first player) adds the edge (1,4), Takahashi (the second player) cannot move. Thus, Aoki wins.

Sample Input 2

4 2
1 2
3 4

Sample Output 2

Takahashi

No matter how Aoki plays, Takahashi wins.

Sample Input 3

9 5
2 9
2 3
4 6
5 7
1 8

Sample Output 3

Aoki

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's model game more abstractly.

We have initial graph bipartite. Let components have bipartition sizes (a_i,b_i), with d_i = a_i - b_i (can choose sign; but component has two possible d_i = ±|d_i|). However the actual set of vertices on each side matters for which sign choices are possible when merging via a specific edge. But maybe for game outcome, only the multiset of absolute imbalances |d_i| matters? Need verify with N=4 path3+isolated: component d_abs=1, isolated d_abs=1. Outcome Aoki. Two disjoint edges: d_abs=0,0 outcome Takahashi. Sample1 d_abs=0 outcome Aoki. Hmm.

Maybe there is a transformation to a game on "tokens" equal to |d_i|? Let's explore.

Consider the final global imbalance D = A-B. Since final graph K_{A,B} must contain initial graph, D = sum ε_i d_i where d_i are intrinsic signed imbalances (choose a reference orientation for each component), ε_i ∈ {±1}? But is every sign combination achievable? For components with both sides nonempty, yes by choosing an edge endpoint side appropriately? Wait if component has both sides nonempty, we can choose orientation to contribute either +d_i or -d_i relative to a global reference? When merging sequentially, the sign of each component relative to final orientation can be chosen independently? I think yes, because when merging a new component into the growing component, we can choose to orient it either same or opposite by selecting an edge endpoint of appropriate side. But if the growing component has both sides nonempty, we can connect to either side to force orientation. If growing component has one side empty? That only happens if it's a single isolated vertex? Actually any connected component with at least one edge has both sides nonempty. The growing component after first merge has at least one edge, so both sides nonempty. Thus for all subsequent merges, both orientations of the new component are possible by choosing edge to a vertex in the appropriate side of the growing component and a vertex in appropriate side of new component. Need the new component also have both sides nonempty to realize both signs? If new component is isolated (b=0), only one side nonempty; but its d_abs=1. Can we realize both ε=+1 and -1 for isolated? Let's check merging isolated vertex into growing component with both sides nonempty. Isolated has only one vertex. To make edge cross, if we connect to growing side A, isolated becomes B; contribution to D = -1 (since isolated's only vertex in B). If connect to growing side B, isolated becomes A; contribution +1. So both signs possible. Thus isolated can be oriented arbitrarily by choosing which side of growing component to connect. Good. What about merging two isolated vertices initially: growing component before merge is just an isolated vertex with one side empty, so not both signs possible; but after first merge, it has both sides. The final orientation of first two isolated vertices: if they are connected, they must be opposite, so their contributions to final D are +1 and -1 (or vice versa), sum 0. Thus the sign combination for two isolated components is constrained: signs opposite, not both same. But if we consider them as two tokens of size1, final D can be 0 only, not ±2. So not all sign combinations independent for isolated components? Wait if there are more components, can two isolated vertices end up same side in final K? They would not have an edge between them, but if they are in different components initially and never merged directly? In final graph K_{A,B}, any two vertices on same side have no edge. But during play, if they are never connected by an edge, they could remain in different components? Terminal requires one component, so eventually they must be connected via paths, but not necessarily directly. If two isolated vertices both end up same side in final K, there is no edge between them, but they can be connected through other vertices? In K_{A,B}, vertices on same side are not adjacent, but the graph is connected if both sides nonempty because every A vertex connects to every B vertex. Two A vertices are connected via any B vertex. So they can be same side. Can a play produce final K_{2,2} from four isolated vertices? Yes, with A={1,3}, B={2,4}; isolated vertices 1 and3 same side, no direct edge. So signs of isolated components can be same in final if they are not the first pair? Let's see four isolated: final D=0 can be achieved by two + and two - contributions (e.g., 1,3 A; 2,4 B). Thus the game is not simply choosing arbitrary signs; players alternate choosing edges, and the orientation of components is determined by the sequence of edge additions. However the final D is determined by the "merge tree" and orientations. We need determine winning strategy.

Maybe there is a simpler invariant: The game is equivalent to playing on a graph where each connected component is a "pile" with size equal to the absolute difference of its bipartition sizes, and a move consists of choosing two piles and replacing them by either |x+y| or |x-y|? Because merging components with imbalances d1,d2 produces new imbalance |d1 ± d2|. Internal moves are passes. The number of internal moves maybe doesn't affect outcome? Wait total moves parity depends on final D, but internal moves count is determined by final D and M. So if we ignore internal moves and consider only merge moves, the parity of total moves = (N^2-D^2)/4 - M. Merge moves count = C-1 (C initial components). Internal moves count = total - (C-1). Since total parity depends on D and M. Thus the merge game alone with payoff parity of D? But players can also choose internal moves, which are like passes that change whose turn it is to make merge moves. However because total moves parity is fixed by final D, passes are accounted. In normal play, if a player can choose to pass (internal move) instead of merging, they can alter the parity of merge moves but total moves parity remains same for a given final D. The winner is determined by total parity, not by who makes last merge. Since passes are moves, they affect turn order. But if final D is chosen through merge moves, the number of internal moves is whatever needed to fill terminal; players cannot choose to pass more than available. However they can choose to take internal moves before completing merges, which doesn't change D but consumes a move. This is equivalent to a "move count" associated with each final D. Since total moves parity is fixed for final D, if a player can force a final D with desired parity, they win. The internal moves are not strategic except they contribute to total parity. But the players' choices of internal moves don't affect final D, so they are just part of the total count. The game is a finite game where each move either changes D (merge) or not (internal). The final D is determined by merge moves. The total number of moves is determined by final D. Thus the outcome is determined by whether the player who controls the final D can choose parity? But both players influence D.

Maybe we can view the game as "players alternately choose edges; the final D is a function of the entire play. The player who makes the last move wins iff total moves odd. Since total moves parity is determined by final D, the game is equivalent to a normal-play game where the payoff is parity of a function of the terminal state. This is a "partizan" no, impartial. We can compute winning/losing states recursively with state = current graph? Too large.

- There is an underlying "constraint graph" of equality/inequality? Actually each edge added imposes inequality between endpoint colors. Wait adding an edge between vertices in different components adds an inequality constraint between variables (colors) that were previously unconstrained. Adding an edge within same component between opposite colors adds an inequality constraint that is already implied by the existing constraints (since they are opposite in the same component), but the edge itself was not present. So it's like adding a redundant inequality constraint (an edge that is logically implied but not in the graph).
- Terminal when the inequality graph is complete bipartite between the two color classes: every pair of opposite colors has an edge, and no same-color edges. In terms of constraints, all possible inequalities between the two final color classes are present.

This is similar to the game of building a complete bipartite graph by adding edges, where some edges are "forced" (implied) but not yet added. The players can add forced edges as "free moves" that don't change the partition.

This is like a game where on a turn, a player may either change the state (merge) or decrement a counter. The outcome is determined by the parity of total moves, which equals f(D). This is equivalent to the merge game with an extra option to "pass" (internal move) that does not change D but consumes a move. Maybe we can transform the game to a game on a forest by considering the "bipartite double cover"? Or by contracting color classes? Let's explore.

In a connected bipartite component with color classes A,B, the missing cross edges are all pairs (a,b) not already edges. Adding any missing cross edge is legal and doesn't change the component's color classes. So within a component, the game is just filling a bipartite graph to complete bipartite. This is equivalent to playing on a complete bipartite graph where some edges are already present; players add missing edges. If there are no other components, the outcome is simply parity of missing edges (since every move fills one and no choices). Indeed sample1 path4 missing=1 -> first. Star missing=0 -> second. If there are multiple components, cross moves can merge and change the bipartition sizes, which changes the number of missing edges.

This suggests a recursive decomposition by connected components: The game is the "disjunctive sum" of the internal filling games plus the "connection game" between components. But internal filling games are just piles of size m_i (missing edges) where a move reduces by 1. In impartial game theory, a pile of size m has SG = m mod 2 (as noted). The connection game between components is a game where a move chooses two components and replaces them by a new component whose internal pile size is some function of the old piles and their color sizes, and also adds 1 to the move count. This is exactly the "selective compound" of piles? Maybe we can compute the SG of a component as a nimber that combines both its internal pile and its "connection value".

Let's define for each component a value that captures its behavior when merged. Suppose a component has color sizes (a,b) and missing internal edges m. If it stands alone, its SG is m mod 2 (since only internal moves). But when merged with another component, the resulting component's m changes. The game on multiple components is not the XOR of individual SGs because a move can merge two components, which is not allowed in disjoint sum. However there is a concept of "join" in impartial games: The "union" of graphs where a move can either play in one component or merge two components is the "join" operation, whose SG is related to the XOR of SGs plus something. For the game of adding edges to a graph while preserving some property, the SG of a disjoint union might be computed by the "Sprague-Grundy function for the join of graphs" known as the "Welter's game"? Let's investigate.

Observation: The game is equivalent to the following: We have a graph G. Let H be the graph whose vertices are the connected components of G? No.

Another angle: Since adding an edge between different components is always legal, the game will not end until the graph is connected. Thus exactly C-1 merge moves will occur, where C is initial number of connected components. The total number of moves = (C-1) + number of internal moves. The number of internal moves is the number of edges added within components. At the end, for each final component (one), all cross pairs of its bipartition are filled. The number of internal moves equals final_edges - M - (C-1). This is determined by final D. So total moves parity = (C-1) + internal_moves parity. But final_edges - M = total moves. So no new.

Could we think of each merge move as "choosing a sign" for one component, and each internal move as "flipping a bit" in a counter. The final D is the sum of signed d_i. The players alternately choose signs, but the order of choosing signs is determined by merge moves; internal moves can be inserted. Since internal moves don't affect D, they just allow a player to "pass" and change who chooses the next sign. In a game where players alternately choose signs for components and the payoff is parity of sum, the outcome might depend only on the multiset of d_i modulo something, not on passes, because passes can be used strategically but their total parity is determined by final sum? Wait passes are not independent; but if a player wants to pass, they need an available internal edge. The number of available internal edges at any time depends on previous merges. However the total number of passes for a given final D is fixed. Thus if a player can force a final D, the number of passes is also fixed, so they cannot choose to pass more or fewer without changing D. Therefore passes are not a separate strategic tool; they are just part of the total move count. The game is essentially: players alternate making moves; each move either adds a constraint (merge) or adds a redundant edge. The final D is determined by the sequence of constraints. The redundant edges are forced by the final D. So the outcome is determined by whether the player who can force the final D also gets the last move? But the last move is just the last edge added; if final D parity yields odd total moves, first wins.

Thus we can ignore the distinction between merge and internal moves and consider the game as a "normal play" where the terminal payoff is f(D). This is a finite game with perfect information and no draws. The SG of the initial state could be computed by considering all possible final D? But players can choose any legal edge, not just final D. However maybe the game is equivalent to the following simpler game: There are C components with weights d_i. On each turn, a player chooses two components and merges them, choosing the sign of the resulting weight (i.e., replacing d_i,d_j by d_i + d_j or d_i - d_j). Additionally, before/during, there are some "free moves" equal to the current number of missing internal edges, but the total number of free moves is determined by the final weight. Is the outcome of this simpler merge game (without internal moves) plus a fixed offset M equal to the original? Let's test.

Define merge game: Start with C components with weights d_i. A move: choose two components with weights x,y and replace them by |x+y| or |x-y| (or signed). After C-1 moves, one weight D remains. The number of moves in merge game is C-1. In original, total moves = (C-1) + internal_moves. internal_moves = final_edges - M - (C-1). This depends on D and also on initial M and C. If we add a "bonus" to the merge game payoff: first wins iff (C-1 + internal_moves) odd. But internal_moves is not a fixed bonus independent of D. However we can define a payoff f(D) = final_edges - M mod2. The merge game's moves are C-1; if we played only merge game, the winner would be determined by parity of C-1 and who makes last merge. But original has extra moves. So not equivalent.

Maybe we can incorporate the internal moves as "passes" in the merge game. In a game with passes, the outcome can be determined by the "normal play" of the merge game with an extra option to pass. The number of passes available at a state is a function of the current component weights and m_i. This is complex.

Now, how do component imbalances combine modulo 4? Each component has an intrinsic imbalance d_i = a_i - b_i (can be positive/negative). When merging components, the new imbalance D = d_i ± d_j (up to sign). Modulo 4, the sign matters for odd d_i. But note d_i parity = component size parity. Maybe there is an invariant modulo 2 of the multiset of d_i/2? Let's define for each component a value:
- If component size n_i is even, d_i is even. Let r_i = d_i/2 mod2.
- If component size n_i is odd, d_i is odd. But total N even means even number of odd components. Merging two odd components yields an even component with d = odd ± odd = even. The resulting r = (d_i ± d_j)/2 mod2. For odd d_i, d_i mod4 is either 1 or 3. The sign can flip 1<->3. So the contribution of an odd component to the final k depends on its orientation (sign). This is like each odd component carries a bit (d_i mod4) that can be flipped.

Maybe the game of merging components modulo 4 is equivalent to the following: Each component has a "parity" p_i = (d_i mod 2) (component size parity) and a "quarter" q_i = floor? When merging:
- even+even: d even ± even = even. k_new = k_i ± k_j mod2? Since d_i/2 mod2 = k_i. d_i ± d_j over 2 mod2 = k_i + k_j mod2 (subtraction same as addition mod2). So k_new = k_i XOR k_j.
- odd+odd: d_i,d_j odd. d_i ± d_j is even. (d_i ± d_j)/2 mod2 depends on d_i mod4 and d_j mod4 and sign. Let's encode odd d as a bit s = (d mod4 ==1 ? 0? or 1). If d mod4 =1 or3. Since sign can flip, the absolute value mod4 matters. But orientation can choose sign, so the effective contribution of an odd component to a sum can be either d or -d. Mod4, -1≡3, -3≡1. Thus an odd component can contribute either 1 or3 mod4 depending on orientation. So its "quarter bit" can be chosen.
- odd+even: d odd ± even = odd. The resulting component is odd. Mod4, d_new = d_odd ± d_even. Since d_even is 0 or2 mod4. Adding/subtracting 2 flips the odd component's mod4 between 1 and3. So merging odd with even can flip the odd component's orientation bit.

This suggests a game of bits where odd components are "tokens" that can be flipped by merging with even components, and merging two odd components annihilates them producing an even component with k = something.

Let's consider the merge game without passes. Start with C components. Each move reduces component count by 1. There are C-1 moves. For a component with odd d_i (odd size), d_i mod4 is either 1 or 3. Let's define its contribution to D mod4 as s_i * r_i where r_i = d_i mod4 ∈ {1,3}, and s_i ∈ {±1} is the sign. However for determining the winner, we can define the state as a multiset of groups, each with a size (number of odd components) and an x value (XOR of s_i within group). Initially all x=0. A move merges two groups A,B; the player can choose the new x_new from the set:
S(A,B) = { x_A XOR x_B XOR t : t ∈ {0,  |A| mod2, |B| mod2, |A| mod2 XOR |B| mod2? } }
Let's derive more carefully.

When merging A and B, we add an edge with label l. The final signs of all vertices in B relative to A are toggled by l if we choose root in A, or all vertices in A toggled by l if root in B. The internal XOR of the new group relative to some root:
- If root in A: x_new = x_A XOR (x_B XOR (l * (|B| mod2))) = x_A XOR x_B XOR (l * |B| mod2).
- If root in B: x_new = (x_A XOR (l * |A| mod2)) XOR x_B = x_A XOR x_B XOR (l * |A| mod2).
Since l can be 0 or1, the possible x_new values are:
If |A| mod2 = 0 and |B| mod2 = 0: {x_A XOR x_B}.
If |A| mod2 = 0, |B| mod2 = 1: root A gives {x, x XOR1}; root B gives {x} (since |A| even). So possible {x, x XOR1} (because player can choose root A and l=1). Thus can toggle.
If |A| mod2 = 1, |B| mod2 = 0: similarly can toggle.
If |A| mod2 = 1, |B| mod2 = 1: root A gives {x, x XOR1}; root B gives {x, x XOR1}. So can toggle.
Thus the new x can be either x_A XOR x_B or x_A XOR x_B XOR 1, unless both |A| and |B| are even, in which case it is fixed to x_A XOR x_B. Wait if one odd one even, can toggle by choosing root in the odd group? Yes. So the only case where the player cannot toggle the combined x is when both groups have an even number of odd components. This recursive game on groups with sizes (number of odd components) and x values seems tractable! The size of a group is just the number of odd components it contains. Even components have size0. The x value is a bit. The move:
- Choose two groups with sizes a,b and x values p,q.
- New size = a+b.
- New x can be:
   * If a and b are both even: x = p XOR q (fixed).
   * Otherwise: x can be p XOR q or p XOR q XOR 1 (player's choice).
The game ends when one group remains; its x is the final X. The payoff for the first player is whether final X equals some target T (where T = G XOR desired_k). Actually the first player wants final X = T, second wants final X != T. This is a finite impartial game with a boolean terminal condition. We need determine if the first player has a winning strategy.

This is now a much simpler combinatorial game. The state can be summarized by the multiset of group sizes? The x values matter. But note the x value of a group is just a bit; when merging, the new x is p XOR q possibly toggled. This is reminiscent of the game of Nim with heaps of size = group size? Maybe the outcome depends only on the parity of the number of odd-sized groups and the XOR of x values? Let's analyze.

Let's define for each group a pair (size parity, x). Since only parity of size matters for future moves? The new size parity = (a+b) mod2. The ability to toggle depends on whether a or b is odd. So only size parity matters, not the actual count. The number of odd components in a group can be large, but parity is enough for the toggle condition and new size parity. However the total number of groups and the parity of sizes determine the game. The actual sizes beyond parity don't affect the x-game. Great! Because we only care about final X, and the toggle condition uses parity. The number of odd components O is even, so the final group size parity is even (0). The game state can be represented by:
- Number of groups with odd size (call O_groups) and even size (E_groups).
- The XOR of x values over all groups? Let's see.

When merging two groups:
- If both even: new group even. x_new = p XOR q. The total XOR of x over all groups changes from X_total = P XOR Q XOR R to P XOR Q XOR R XOR (p XOR q) = R? Wait before merge, groups A(p), B(q), others R. Total XOR = p XOR q XOR R. After merge, new group x = p XOR q, others R, total XOR = (p XOR q) XOR R = same. So merging two even groups preserves total XOR of x.
- If one odd one even: new group odd. x_new = p XOR q or p XOR q XOR1 (player choice). Total XOR before = p XOR q XOR R. After = x_new XOR R. So the player can either preserve total XOR (if x_new = p XOR q) or toggle it (if x_new = p XOR q XOR1). Thus merging odd+even allows the player to toggle the total XOR of x.
- If both odd: new group even. x_new = p XOR q or p XOR q XOR1. Total XOR before = p XOR q XOR R. After = x_new XOR R. So again player can preserve or toggle total XOR.

Thus in all merges except even+even, the player making the move can choose whether to toggle the total XOR of x. Since the final X is the x of the single final group, which equals the total XOR of x at the end. Initially, all x=0, so total XOR=0. The final X is the total XOR after all merges. Therefore the game reduces to:
- There are groups; initially each odd component is an odd group, each even component is an even group.
- A move merges two groups. The new group's size parity is (a+b) mod2.
- If the two groups are both even, the total XOR is unchanged and the player has no choice.
- Otherwise, the player may choose to toggle the total XOR or not.
The players have opposing goals on the final total XOR.

This is much simpler! The actual x values of individual groups don't matter except through the total XOR, because the player can always choose to toggle or not when merging any non-even-even pair, regardless of p,q. Wait is that true? If both odd, x_new can be p XOR q or p XOR q XOR1, so total XOR toggled or not. Yes. If odd+even, same. Thus the only thing that matters is the sequence of merges and which pairs are even-even. The total XOR can be toggled by a player exactly when they make a merge that is not even+even.

Thus the game is equivalent to: We have a process of merging items with parity (odd/even). Each move selects two items and replaces them by an item with parity XOR. This is now a finite game where the players control bits on certain moves. The number of non-even-even merges and who controls them depends on the sequence. But note:
- Merging odd+odd produces an even item. This reduces the number of odd items by 2.
- Merging odd+even produces an odd item. Number of odd items unchanged.
- Merging even+even produces an even item. Number of odd items unchanged.
The total number of odd items O is even initially. The process ends with 0 odd items (since final component size N even? Wait if N even, final component size even, so odd items 0. If N odd, we handled separately; for even N, yes O even and final 0).

The players can choose which items to merge. This is similar to the game of reducing a multiset of parities. The number of non-even-even merges:
- Each odd+odd merge reduces O by 2 and gives a choice.

We need determine if first can force the XOR of choices to equal T. This is a "boolean game" where choices are available on certain moves. Let's define a "choice move" as a non-even-even merge. On a choice move, the player can choose the bit. The final XOR is the XOR of all choice bits. Since each choice bit is independent and controlled by the player who makes that move, the final XOR is the XOR of bits chosen by Aoki on his choice moves and bits chosen by Takahashi on his choice moves. Aoki wants the total XOR = T. Takahashi wants != T.

This is exactly a game where players alternately make moves, and some moves allow the player to set a bit. The final XOR is the XOR of all set bits. If the total number of choice moves is K, then the final XOR is determined by the bits. The player who makes the last choice move can set the final XOR to whatever they want, because they can choose their bit to make the total T or not, regardless of previous bits. Therefore the outcome is determined by who makes the last choice move! Is that true? Suppose there are K choice moves. Let previous XOR before last choice be P. The last player can choose bit b, final XOR = P XOR b. If the last player is Aoki, he wants T, so sets b = P XOR T. If last player is Takahashi, he wants not T, so sets b = P XOR (1-T) (i.e., final = 1-T). Thus the player who makes the last choice move can force the outcome. Therefore the winner is simply the player who makes the last non-even-even merge. Even-even merges don't affect the choice count and just pass the turn.

This is a huge simplification! Let's verify with examples.

- star: one even item, no choice moves, final X fixed. Outcome determined.

Excellent! So the game reduces to: The player who makes the last "choice move" (merge involving at least one odd-sized component) wins, because they can set the final X to their desired value. - OE to (4,1), choice. Need AokiWin(next, Takahashi) for O=4,E=1. We need compute O=4,E=1 Takahashi turn.
Let's compute O=4,E=1 Takahashi turn:
State (4,1), Takahashi. Legal:
  * OO to (2,2), choice. After move, Aoki turn O=2,E=2. AokiWin from Aoki turn O=2,E=2 = True. Takahashi wants False; can choose bit R' to make AokiWin false? AokiWin from Aoki turn O=2,E=2 is True for all R (since Aoki can OO to O=0 and choose R'=0). So OO cannot make Aoki lose.
  * OE to (4,0), choice. After move, Aoki turn O=4,E=0. AokiWin from Aoki turn O=4,E=0 = False (we found). Takahashi can choose bit R' such that AokiWin(next, Aoki)=False. Since next AokiWin is False for all R', OE is winning for Takahashi. Thus AokiWin from Takahashi turn O=4,E=1 = False.
So OE move from Aoki turn O=4,E=2 leads to next state O=4,E=1, Takahashi turn, AokiWin=False. Thus OE fails.
- EE to (4,1), no choice. Next state O=4,E=1, Takahashi turn, AokiWin=False. So EE also fails.
Thus Aoki turn O=4,E=2 loses.

O=4,E=3. Aoki turn:
- OO to (2,4), choice. Need O=2,E=4 Takahashi turn = False. fails.
- OE to (4,2), choice. Need O=4,E=2 Takahashi turn. Let's compute O=4,E=2 Takahashi turn.
State (4,2), Takahashi.
  * OO to (2,3), choice. After move, Aoki turn O=2,E=3. AokiWin from Aoki turn O=2,E=3 = True (since Aoki can OO to O=0 and choose R'=0). Takahashi cannot make false? True for all R. So OO not winning for Takahashi.
  * OE to (4,1), choice. After move, Aoki turn O=4,E=1. AokiWin from Aoki turn O=4,E=1 = True (we found). True for all R? Let's verify O=4,E=1 Aoki turn: Aoki can OE to (4,0) and next Takahashi turn O=4,E=0 AokiWin=True, so yes True for all R. So OE not winning.
  * EE to (4,1), no choice. Next Aoki turn O=4,E=1, AokiWin=True. So not winning.
Thus Takahashi has no winning move; AokiWin from Takahashi turn O=4,E=2 = True.
So OE move from Aoki turn O=4,E=3 leads to next state O=4,E=2, Takahashi turn, AokiWin=True. Thus Aoki can win. So O=4,E=3 Aoki wins.

Pattern for O=4: Aoki wins iff E is odd? E=0 lose, E=1 win, E=2 lose, E=3 win. Let's test O=4,E=0 empty4 lose; with one even component (K2+4iso) E=1 would Aoki win? Earlier I guessed second might win, but according to this pattern Aoki wins! Let's verify with the game for N=6, O=4,E=1 (K2 + four isolated). We thought second could pair odd components, but maybe Aoki has a winning strategy using OE merge. Let's simulate to confirm.

State: K2 (even), four isolated (odd). O=4,E=1, Aoki turn. According to DP, Aoki should merge an odd with the even (OE) to reach O=4,E=0 on Takahashi's turn, which is losing for Takahashi (Aoki wins). Let's see.

Now we need general pattern for AokiWin from initial Aoki turn as a function of O and E. Let's compute more.

We can try to derive recurrence and solve.

Let A(O,E) = AokiWin from Aoki turn (initial) for the x-game, ignoring R? But we saw for O=0, AokiWin depends on R (T). For O>0, it seemed independent of R? Let's check: For O=2, Aoki turn True for all R; Takahashi turn False for all R. For O=4,E=0, Aoki turn False for all R; Takahashi turn True for all R. For O=4,E=1, Aoki turn True for all R; Takahashi turn False for all R? We found O=4,E=1 Takahashi turn False for all R. So for O>0, the outcome may be independent of R because the player who makes the last choice can always set the final R to desired value regardless of previous R. Indeed if there is at least one choice move, the last choice mover can set final R. Thus the only relevant question is who makes the last choice move. The x-game outcome is independent of T when O>0. If O=0, no choice moves, outcome depends on T. This matches our earlier "last choice mover wins" insight.

Therefore for O>0, AokiWin is simply whether Aoki can force that he makes the last choice move. We can compute a boolean L(O,E) = True if the player to move can force that they make the last choice move. This is a normal-play game where making a choice move does not end the game, but the player who makes the last choice wins. We can define L for the player to move. Terminal with no moves: if no choice moves have occurred? But if O=0,E=1, there are no moves, and the last choice mover was determined earlier. In the recursive definition, L(terminal) should be False for the player to move (they cannot make a choice). However if there are no choice moves at all, the "last choice" doesn't exist; but for O>0, there is at least one choice move before terminal. We can define L(state) = True if the player to move can force that they make the last choice move. Recurrence:
- A move that is a choice (OO or OE): The current player makes a choice, but may not be the last. After the move, the opponent is to move in next state. The current player will make the last choice iff the opponent cannot force that they (opponent) make the last choice, i.e., L(next) = False. Since the current player made a choice, if no later choices occur, current is the last choice mover. So a choice move is winning for current iff L(next) = False.
- A move that is no-choice (EE): Current player does not make a choice. They will make the last choice iff in the next state, the opponent cannot force that they make the last choice, i.e., L(next) = False. Same condition! Wait if current makes no-choice and no later choices, the last choice was made before current's turn, so current cannot be the last choice mover. But if L(next)=False, that means the opponent to move cannot force that they make the last choice. Does that imply current will make the last choice? Let's think. Suppose state has no choice moves left (O=0,E>1). L(next) for opponent? For O=0, there are no choice moves ever, so no one can make a last choice. The concept of "current player can force they make the last choice" is False for both players. If current makes an EE move to O=0,E-1, L(next)=False. If we used condition L(next)=False, we would incorrectly say current wins. But actually no one makes a last choice; the winner is determined by fixed X, not by last choice. Thus for the last-choice game, we should only consider states where at least one choice move will occur in the future. If O=0, there are no future choice moves, so the last choice mover is already determined by history, and the recursive L cannot be defined solely from state. However in the original game, if O=0 from the start, there are no choice moves and outcome is fixed. If O>0 initially, then at least one choice move will occur. But a player might make an EE move when O>0; there are still future choice moves, so L(next) is meaningful. Once O=0, no future choices; the last choice has already been made. The player to move at O=0 cannot make the last choice, so L(O=0,E) should be False for the player to move. But the current player in a state with O>0 considering an EE move to O=0? If no later choices, current is the last choice mover, so the move is winning for current regardless of L(next). Our condition for choice move: winning iff L(next)=False. For O=0, L(next)=False, so it works. Good. For no-choice EE, O remains >0 (since O>0), so there will be future choices; L(next) is defined. If L(next)=False, does that mean current will make the last choice? Suppose current makes EE, opponent to move in next state cannot force that they make the last choice. Since there will be at least one future choice, the last choice will be made by someone. If opponent cannot force it, then current can force it (by optimal play from next state? Actually after current's EE, it's opponent's turn. If L(next)=False, that means no matter what opponent does, current will make the last choice. So yes, current wins. Thus the recurrence L(state) = exists legal move such that L(next)=False works for both choice and no-choice moves! This is exactly the normal-play winning condition where a move to a P-position is winning. Terminal O=0,E=1 has no moves, L=False. For O=0,E>1, only EE moves to O=0,E-1, L(next)=False, so L would become True, which is wrong because no choices. But if O=0 from the start, there are no choice moves and the "last choice" concept doesn't determine winner. However in our original game with O=0, we handle separately. For O>0, the recurrence should be valid because there is at least one choice move in the future. But what about a state with O>0 and E large where a player could make EE moves until O=0? EE doesn't change O, so O remains >0 until an OO move reduces it. Thus O=0 only reached by OO choice move. So L(next) for O=0 is False, and the OO move is winning. Good.

Thus for O>0, L(state) can be computed by normal-play rules on the state (O,E), where legal moves:
- OO: to (O-2, E+1)
- OE: to (O, E-1) if E>=1
- EE: to (O, E-1) if E>=2
Terminal states with no moves? The game ends when total items=1. If O>0 and total items=1 impossible because O even and N even? Could have O=0,E=1. So terminal O=0,E=1 has L=False. Also if O>0 and total items=1? O=1,E=0 but O even, so no. Thus terminal only O=0,E=1.

We want L(initial O,E) for O>0. Let's compute this recurrence. It is exactly the normal-play outcome of a game where players merge items and the player who makes the last move? Wait normal play: player unable to move loses. Here terminal L=False. The moves are the same as the original merge game but ignoring bits. The player who makes the last move in this merge game is not necessarily the last choice mover? Actually every move in this reduced game is a merge; the last move is the final merge to one item. Therefore the player who makes the last move in the reduced game is the last choice mover. Great! So for O>0, the winner of the original x-game (who controls last choice) is exactly the winner of the normal-play game on items with parities, where a move merges two items, and the player unable to move loses. This is much simpler.

- OO (O>=2): next (O-2, E+1). If O-2=0, L=False, so OO is winning.

Now we need map this back to original problem.

Let's summarize the solution for even N:

1. Compute connected components of initial graph. For each component, determine:
   - n_i = number of vertices.
   - a_i, b_i = sizes of the two color classes (bipartition). Since graph is bipartite, we can 2-color each component.
   - d_i = a_i - b_i (can be positive/negative; choose one coloring).
   - O = number of components with odd n_i (equiv odd d_i).
   - E = number of components with even n_i.
   - If O==0: final X = 0 (no odd components, no orientation choices affecting k? Wait even components have fixed q_i; final k = G = Q XOR P XOR B, with P=O/2=0, B=0, so k = Q. Q = XOR over even components of q_i = (d_i/2 mod2). Since no odd components, final D = sum ± d_i but d_i even, sign doesn't change d_i mod4? Actually d_i even, sign doesn't change d_i/2 mod2, so final k = XOR q_i. Yes. So k = Q. Then total moves parity = product_parity(k) XOR M_parity. Aoki wins iff total moves odd.
   - If O>0:
      * If O==2: Aoki wins (because he can force last choice).
      * If O>=4: Aoki wins iff E is odd; if E even, Takahashi wins.
But wait is this independent of M and T? Yes, because the player who makes the last choice can set final X to make total moves odd/even as needed. But what if the desired target T is such that the last choice mover cannot set final X? They can always set final X to any bit by choosing their last toggle bit. So yes, the last choice mover wins regardless of M. Thus for O>0, the winner is simply the last choice mover: Aoki if (O==2) or (O>=4 and E odd), else Takahashi.

Let's test with all N=4 states:
- empty4: O=4,E=0 => O>=4, E even => Takahashi. Matches.
- K2+2iso: O=2 => Aoki. Matches.
- K2+K2: O=0 => need compute Q. Components K2 each d=0 q=0, Q=0. N=4, M=2. Product parity for k=0: N≡0 mod4 => product parity=k=0. Total parity=0 XOR M_parity(0)=0 even => Takahashi. Matches.
- P3+iso: O=2 => Aoki. Matches.
- path4: O=0, Q=0, M=3 => product parity0, total parity1 => Aoki. Matches.
- star: O=0, component d=-2 (a=1,b=3) q = d/2 mod2 = -1 mod2 =1. Q=1. k=1. N≡0 mod4 product parity=1. M=3 => total parity=1 XOR1=0 => Takahashi. Matches.

Great.

N=6 examples:
- Empty N=6: O=6,E=0, O>=4, E even => Takahashi. N odd? N=6 even. Total moves even? For N=6 empty, possible terminal edges: K_{3,3} edges9 odd? Wait N=6, A+B=6. Now we need handle N odd separately. Earlier we said for N odd, terminal edges A*B is always even, so total moves parity = M mod2. Thus Aoki wins iff M is odd. Let's verify with our general framework? For N odd, final component has odd size, so O? The final component size odd, so the number of odd components O is odd? Wait total N odd, sum of component sizes odd, so number of odd-size components O is odd. Our x-game assumed O even because final D even. For N odd, D is odd, k not defined. The parity of terminal edges is even regardless, so no choices matter. So answer is simply M parity. Need confirm with N=1 M=0: M even => Takahashi. Good.

But wait, is it always true that for N odd, every terminal complete bipartite graph has even number of edges? A+B=N odd => one of A,B even, the other odd, product even. Yes. Therefore total moves = even - M, parity = M mod2. Since M parity fixed, winner determined. Even if players can choose final A,B, product always even. So yes.

Now we need compute for each connected component:
- n_i (size)
- color class sizes a_i,b_i
- d_i = a_i - b_i
- q_i for even components: q_i = (d_i / 2) mod 2. Since d_i can be negative, in Python modulo 2 of negative? We need q_i = (abs(d_i)//2) % 2? Let's check: d_i even. d_i mod4 can be 0 or2. q_i = 1 if d_i ≡ 2 mod4, 0 if d_i ≡0 mod4. Since sign doesn't matter: -2 ≡ 2 mod4, so abs(d_i)//2 %2 works. For d_i=-2, abs=2, //2=1, %2=1. Good.
- O = number of components with n_i odd.
- E = number of components with n_i even.

For O==0, compute Q = XOR of q_i over all components (even components only, but all are even). Then k = Q. Need determine if Aoki wins:
total_moves_parity = product_parity(k) XOR (M % 2)
where product_parity = (A*B) % 2 for final k. Need formula for N even.

Let's derive product_parity in terms of k and N mod4.

N even. Let N = 2n0. k = (D/2) mod2. A = N/2 + D/2 = n0 + (D/2). B = n0 - (D/2). Let h = D/2. h parity = k. A*B = n0^2 - h^2. Parity:
- If n0 even (N divisible by 4): n0^2 even, so parity = h^2 mod2 = k.
- If n0 odd (N ≡2 mod4): n0^2 odd, so parity = 1 - k (mod2).
Thus product_parity = k if N%4==0 else 1-k.

Aoki wins iff (product_parity ^ (M&1)) == 1.

For O>0:
- If O == 2: Aoki wins.
- Else (O >= 4): Aoki wins iff E % 2 == 1. If E even, Takahashi wins.

But wait, what if O>0 and E? Our L pattern for O>=4: Aoki wins iff E odd. What about O=0? handled. What about O=2 always Aoki, including E any. Let's double-check O=2,E=0 (two odd components, no even) e.g., N=2 empty or N=4 P3+iso? P3+iso has O=2,E=0? P3 size3 odd, isolated size1 odd, E=0. Aoki wins. N=2 empty O=2,E=0 Aoki wins. Good.
O=2,E=1 (K2+2iso) Aoki wins. O=2,E=2? Example N=6: two odd components + two even components. According to rule Aoki wins. Let's test with DP: O=2,E=2, L=True. Aoki can merge the two odd components on first move, making O=0,E=3, and he made the last choice? Wait after merging OO, O=0, no future choices, so Aoki made the last choice. Yes, he wins. So O=2 always Aoki by immediately merging the two odd components if they exist? But what if the two odd components are not directly mergeable? Any two components can be merged by adding an edge between them, so yes. If O=2, Aoki can merge them on his first move, making a single even component (plus other evens). This is a choice move and the last choice move, so he can set final X to desired target. Then the rest are no-choice EE merges, which don't change X. Thus Aoki wins. Great.

For O>=4, why E odd? Let's derive strategy:
- If E odd, Aoki can merge an odd with an even (OE) on first move, leaving O same, E even, and it's Takahashi's turn in a state with O>=4, E even, which is losing for the player to move (Takahashi cannot force last choice). This matches DP.
- If E even, any move Aoki makes leads to a state with E? Let's see:
   * OO: O-=2, E+=1 -> E becomes odd. If O-2>=2? If O=4, O-2=2, and O=2 is winning for player to move (Takahashi) regardless of E. If O>=6, state O>=4, E odd, which is winning for player to move (Takahashi). So bad.
   * OE: O same, E-=1 -> E odd (since E even -> E-1 odd). State O>=4, E odd winning for Takahashi.
   * EE: E-=1 -> E odd. State O>=4, E odd winning for Takahashi.
Thus Takahashi can force win. Nice.

Now, let's prove the L pattern for O>=4: L = (E odd). We can present in final solution? The final answer only code, but maybe no explanation needed. But we need be confident.

Let's derive L recurrence formally:
Let L(O,E) be True if player to move can force making the last non-even-even merge (choice move). Define L(0,E)=False for all E (no future choice moves). For O>0:
- If O=2: OO move leads to O=0, which is False, so L=True.
- If O>=4:
   * If E is odd: OE move leads to (O, E-1) with E-1 even. Need L(O, even) = False for O>=4. We can prove by induction that for O>=4, L(O,E) = (E odd). Base O=4 we computed. Inductive step:
       - If E odd: OE to (O, E-1) even, which by induction is False, so L=True.
       - If E even: any move:
           OO -> (O-2, E+1). If O-2=2, L(2, E+1)=True; if O-2>=4, E+1 odd, by induction L=True.
           OE -> (O, E-1) with E-1 odd, L=True.
           EE -> (O, E-1) odd, L=True.
         So no move to False, L=False.
This induction works. For O=2, L=True regardless of E because OO to O=0 False. For O=0, L=False.

Thus final rule for O>0:
- O==2 -> Aoki.
- O>=4 -> Aoki iff E odd.

Now, implementation details for N even:

We need compute component sizes and color class sizes. We can use adjacency list. Since N up to 2e5, M up to 2e5, recursion limit maybe set high but iterative safer.

Pseudo:

import sys
def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data: return
    it = iter(data)
    N = next(it); M = next(it)
    if N % 2 == 1:
        print("Aoki" if M % 2 == 1 else "Takahashi")
        return
    adj = [[] for _ in range(N)]
    for _ in range(M):
        u = next(it)-1; v = next(it)-1
        adj[u].append(v); adj[v].append(u)
    color = [-1]*N
    O = 0
    E = 0
    Q = 0
    for i in range(N):
        if color[i] == -1:
            # BFS
            stack = [i]
            color[i] = 0
            cnt = [0,0]
            cnt[0] = 1
            while stack:
                u = stack.pop()
                for v in adj[u]:
                    if color[v] == -1:
                        color[v] = color[u] ^ 1
                        cnt[color[v]] += 1
                        stack.append(v)
            n = cnt[0]+cnt[1]
            if n % 2 == 1:
                O += 1
            else:
                E += 1
                d = cnt[0] - cnt[1]
                # d even
                q = (abs(d)//2) & 1
                Q ^= q
    if O > 0:
        if O == 2:
            print("Aoki")
        else: # O >=4
            print("Aoki" if E % 2 == 1 else "Takahashi")
    else:
        # all components even
        k = Q
        if N % 4 == 0:
            prod_parity = k
        else:
            prod_parity = 1 - k
        total_parity = prod_parity ^ (M & 1)
        print("Aoki" if total_parity == 1 else "Takahashi")

Wait, internal moves are not part of the merge game; they are no-choice moves that don't change X. They can occur before, between, or after merges. In our x-game, we ignored internal moves entirely. However players could make internal moves instead of merge moves, changing the turn order of merges. Did we account for this? This is critical!

In the original game, at any state, a player may choose an internal edge (within a component) instead of a merge. Internal moves don't change O,E or X, but they consume a turn. In our reduced merge game, we ignored internal moves. Could a player use internal moves to alter who makes the last choice move? Earlier we said the total number of moves parity is determined by final k, and the last choice mover can set k. But internal moves are part of total moves; however they don't affect X. We need to incorporate internal moves (passes) into the game. This is crucial. Let's revisit.

In the original game, a move is either:
- Merge (changes O,E, and is a choice move if non-EE).
- Internal (does not change O,E,X, no choice; just reduces the number of missing internal edges).

Internal moves are not unlimited; their count depends on current components and final k. But at any state, the number of available internal moves is m_total = sum (a_i b_i - e_i). This can be large. A player can choose an internal move as a pass. The total number of internal moves in the whole game is determined by final k, but the timing is flexible. In impartial game theory, a pile of passes of size W has SG = W mod2 if it's a separate heap, but here passes are embedded and can be taken anytime.

n^2 - d^2 over 4. Since n and d same parity. If n even, d even; if n odd, d odd.
For n odd: n^2≡1 mod8? d^2≡1 mod8, difference divisible by8? Actually n,d odd => n^2≡1 mod8, d^2≡1 mod8, difference ≡0 mod8, so (n^2-d^2)/4 is even. Thus for odd n, a*b is even? In impartial games, if a position has a move that just decrements a counter (a "waiting move"), and all other moves change the state, the SG can be computed as mex of options. A counter of size m where the only move is to m-1 has SG = m mod2. If the counter is part of the state and other moves also change the counter, the SG is not simply XOR. Let's compute SG for small states with m to see pattern. We can use the recursive definition of AokiWin (boolean) rather than full SG. Since we only need win/loss, maybe the outcome is determined by the parity of (M + something). Let's derive from total moves parity again.

Total moves parity = final_edges - M mod2. final_edges parity depends on k. The players can influence k through choice moves. Internal moves don't affect k but they affect the total move count directly. However the total move count is exactly final_edges - M. If a player can force a particular k, the total move count parity is fixed. The winner is determined by k. The strategic game is about choosing k. Internal moves are just part of the total count; they don't change k. But they do affect whose turn it is when a choice move is made. However the total number of moves is fixed once k is fixed, so the parity of the move number of the last choice is not independent; it's part of the total. But if a player wants to set k to a certain value, they need to be the one making the last choice. Internal moves can change who makes the last choice. So we need determine who can force the last choice in the presence of internal moves.

This is exactly the game we started with. Maybe the outcome can be determined by the parity of the total number of moves in the "canonical" play? Let's think differently.

Since total moves parity = final_edges - M, and final_edges parity depends only on k, the game is equivalent to a game where the payoff is k. The players can choose k only on choice moves. Internal moves are "passes" that don't affect k. In such games, the player who makes the last choice can set k, but the availability of passes can determine who makes the last choice. This is analogous to a game where there are a number of "choice points" and a number of passes. The passes are not unlimited; their total number is determined by the final k, but maybe the parity of passes is also determined by k and current state. Let's derive the parity of the number of internal moves in terms of k and initial state.

Total internal moves = total moves - merges = (final_edges - M) - (C-1).
final_edges = (N^2 - D^2)/4. D = 2h, h parity = k. So final_edges mod2 = product_parity(k). But we need the exact number modulo 2, which is product_parity(k). Merges count C-1 fixed! Wait total merges is always C-1, regardless of choices. Yes, the number of merge moves is fixed = C-1. Therefore total internal moves parity = total_moves_parity XOR (C-1 mod2) = (product_parity(k) XOR M_parity) XOR ((C-1)&1).

This is determined by k. So if a player can force k, the parity of internal moves is fixed. The number of internal moves is not an independent pass heap; it's a function of k. Thus the game is entirely about forcing k. The internal moves are just filler whose count is determined by k. The players cannot choose to pass more or fewer without changing k. Therefore the strategic game is exactly the merge game with the payoff k, and internal moves are not strategic choices—they are forced in number. But the timing of internal moves can still affect who makes the last choice, but the total parity already accounts for them. If the last choice mover sets k, the total parity is determined; the timing doesn't matter. The question remains: who can force the last choice? The merge game DP without passes assumed players always make merge moves, but they could insert internal moves. However inserting an internal move changes the turn order for merges, but also changes the total move count by 1. Since the total move count parity is determined by k, inserting an internal move early doesn't change the final total parity; it just shifts which player makes subsequent merges. Could a player use this to become the last choice mover? The merge game DP with passes should be equivalent to the merge game DP where the number of passes is not fixed but determined by k. But since k is chosen by the last choice mover, the passes are endogenous.

Let's compute the actual winner for O>0 using a different approach: Since the last choice mover can set k, and internal moves are just part of total parity, perhaps the winner is determined by the parity of the total number of moves in the "canonical" game where all merges happen first and then internal moves. If a player can force being the last choice mover in the merge game, they win. Internal moves can be used to adjust turn order, but the total number of internal moves is determined by k. However the player who controls the last choice can choose k, which determines the parity of internal moves. This is a fixed point: The last choice mover wants total parity odd (if Aoki) or even (if Takahashi). They can choose k to achieve that. The number of internal moves before the last choice is not fixed, but the total parity is. The turn order of merges can be adjusted by internal moves, but the total parity condition might make the merge game outcome independent of internal moves? Let's test with O=4,E=0,m=1: merge game without passes said Takahashi wins (Aoki loses). With one internal move, Aoki wins. So internal moves did change the merge game outcome. But note the internal move itself is a move; if Aoki passes, the total number of moves increases by1, which changes the parity of the move number of the last choice. The merge game DP without passes assumed the last choice occurs on a certain move number; adding a pass shifts it. So the outcome depends on the parity of the number of internal moves before the last choice, which is part of total parity. Since total parity is determined by k, and k is chosen by the last choice mover, there is a self-consistency.

Let's model the game as follows: The players make a sequence of moves. Some moves are choice moves (non-EE merges) where the current player can set a bit that contributes to k. The final k is the XOR of these bits. The total number of moves (including internal and EE merges) is determined by k. The player who makes the last choice can set the final bit to make k any value. Therefore, if a player can ensure they make the last choice, they win. The ability to make the last choice depends on the total number of moves before and including it, which depends on k (because internal moves count depends on k). But the player who makes the last choice can choose k after all previous bits are set; the number of internal moves already made before the last choice is known, but the number of internal moves after the last choice is determined by k. The total parity condition is exactly that the total number of moves is odd/even. The last choice mover can choose k to satisfy this, regardless of how many internal moves occurred before. Thus the only question is whether they can make the last choice. Internal moves before the last choice can shift the turn, but the players can also use internal moves after the last choice? After the last choice, there may be internal moves and EE merges; those don't affect k. The game continues until terminal. The last choice mover wins if total parity is favorable, which they ensure by choosing k. So they don't care about post-choice moves.

Thus the game reduces to: Who can force making the last non-EE merge? Internal moves before the last choice are passes that can be used by either player. The number of internal moves before the last choice is not fixed, but a player can always choose to make an internal move if available. This is exactly the merge game with an additional option to pass, where passes are limited by the current m. But m changes with merges. However, maybe the parity of m before any merge is the only thing that matters, because players can use internal moves as passes and the last choice mover can choose k to make the remaining internal moves parity whatever. Let's test with O=4,E=0,m=1: Aoki can pass, making m=0, then Takahashi to move in merge game with no passes. The merge game with no passes from Takahashi turn is losing for Takahashi, so Aoki wins. This is equivalent to: the merge game outcome for the player to move is L(O,E). If L(O,E)=False (player to move loses), then if m>0, the player can pass to the opponent with m-1. If m-1=0 and L(opponent)=False, then passing wins. In general, with a pass counter m, the outcome is L(O,E) XOR (m mod2)? Let's test:
- O=4,E=0, L(Aoki turn)=False. m=0 -> False. m=1 -> True. m=2 -> False. Yes XOR m mod2.
- O=2,E=0, L(Aoki turn)=True. m=0 -> True. m=1 -> True XOR1 = False? Would Aoki lose if m=1? Let's test O=2,E=0,m=1. State: two odd components, one internal missing edge. Aoki can merge the two odd components immediately and make last choice, so he should win regardless of m. Our XOR formula would say if m=1, Aoki loses, which seems wrong. Let's simulate: Aoki first, if he merges OO, he makes a choice move. After that, O=0, no more choice moves. He set k. Even though there is an internal move (the one m) maybe before or after? If he merges first, the internal move is still available in the merged component or maybe the internal move was in one component before merge; after merge, it's still a missing edge. The total moves parity is determined by k; Aoki wins. So O=2 is always winning regardless of m. Thus simple XOR with m fails because Aoki can win immediately without needing passes. The pass counter only matters if the merge game is losing for the player to move; if winning, they can just play the winning merge move.

More generally, if the merge game is N (winning for player to move), they can make a winning merge move immediately, regardless of passes. If the merge game is P (losing), they may use a pass to flip the turn if m>0. But after passing, the opponent faces the same merge game with m-1. This is exactly the normal-play game where the merge game is a subgame and passes are a heap of size m. The SG of the combined game would be SG_merge XOR (m mod2) if the merge game and pass heap were independent. But if SG_merge is nonzero (N), XOR with m could be zero for some m, which would mean a winning merge position can become losing due to passes. Is that possible? In impartial games, adding a Nim heap of size1 to a winning position can make it losing if SG_merge =1. But in our game, the pass heap is not independent because a merge move also changes m. However if we treat the pass heap as a separate component where a move reduces m by1, and the merge game as another component where moves also change m, then it's not a disjunctive sum. But maybe the SG of the whole state is indeed SG_merge XOR (m mod2) where SG_merge is computed for the merge game with m=0? Let's test O=2,E=0. What is SG_merge? We only computed boolean L. Need SG. If SG_merge = 1 (N), then XOR with m=1 gives 0, predicting losing, which contradicts Aoki can win by merging. But perhaps the merge move from O=2,E=0,m=1 leads to a state with m' not equal to the m of the merge game with m=0. The SG of the whole state is not simply XOR.

Let's compute the actual outcome for O=2,E=0,m=1 to be sure. Construct two odd components, one with m=1, one with m=0. Aoki first. If Aoki merges them, he makes the only choice move (OO) and sets k. After the merge, the graph is connected (one component) with some m'. There are no more choice moves. The game continues with internal moves only. Total moves parity = final_edges - M. Aoki chose k at the merge to make this odd. Therefore Aoki wins. So yes, Aoki wins. Thus passes cannot turn an N merge position into losing if the player can win immediately. In normal play, if SG_merge=1 and m=1, XOR=0 would be losing, but the winning move in the merge game is still available and leads to a terminal payoff that the player can set. The issue is that the "pass heap" is not independent; the merge move changes the pass heap size in a way that preserves the winning condition.

Let's compute m parity for a component in terms of its color sizes and edge count:
m_i = a_i b_i - e_i.
p_i = (a_i b_i) mod2 XOR (e_i mod2).

For a component:
- If n_i odd: a_i b_i even, so p_i = e_i mod2.
- If n_i even: a_i b_i parity = (n_i/2 mod2) XOR q_i, where q_i = (d_i/2 mod2). So p_i = (n_i/2 mod2) XOR q_i XOR e_i mod2.

When merging components i,j with an edge, new m = m_i + m_j + cross -1, where cross is the number of cross edges between the two color classes under the chosen orientation. The parity of cross depends on orientation and color sizes:
- Same orientation: cross = a_i b_j + a_j b_i. Parity = (a_i mod2)(b_j mod2) XOR (a_j mod2)(b_i mod2).
- Opposite orientation: cross = a_i a_j + b_i b_j. Parity = (a_i mod2)(a_j mod2) XOR (b_i mod2)(b_j mod2).
The player making the merge can choose orientation, thus can choose the parity of cross? Let's see. For two components, the parity of cross can be either of two values. Is the player free to choose? Yes, by choosing which side to connect, as long as required vertices exist. For isolated components, maybe constrained. But in terms of parity, maybe both values are possible except some cases.

The new m parity p_new = p_i XOR p_j XOR cross_parity XOR 1 (since -1 mod2 =1).

Let's derive.

Let m_state be total missing internal edges. An internal move reduces m by1. A merge changes m to m' = m_i + m_j + cross -1, so the change in total m is Δ = cross -1 (since m_i+m_j removed, new m includes them plus cross-1). Thus each merge increases m by (cross-1). The total number of internal moves made over the game is:
I = initial m + sum_merges (cross_e - 1) - final m (0)
= initial m + sum_merges (cross_e - 1).
The parity of I = initial m_parity XOR (sum over merges (cross_e -1) mod2).
This is determined by the merge orientations. The total moves parity = (C-1) + I mod2 = (C-1) XOR initial m_parity XOR sum(cross_e -1) mod2.

Let's compute cross_e -1 parity for a merge. cross_e is the number of cross edges between the two merged components under the chosen orientation. The new m increases by cross_e -1. This is the number of new internal missing edges created by the merge (excluding the added edge). The parity of cross_e -1 is cross_parity XOR 1. The player can choose orientation, thus choose cross_parity. Is cross_parity always freely choosable? Let's examine.

For two components with color sizes (a1,b1), (a2,b2). Same orientation cross = a1 b2 + a2 b1. Opposite orientation cross = a1 a2 + b1 b2. The parities:
P_same = (a1&1)*(b2&1) XOR (a2&1)*(b1&1)
P_opp = (a1&1)*(a2&1) XOR (b1&1)*(b2&1)
Are P_same and P_opp always different? Let's test:
- Both components odd (n odd): each has one side odd, one even. Suppose a1 odd,b1 even; a2 odd,b2 even. P_same = 1*0 XOR 1*0 =0. P_opp = 1*1 XOR 0*0 =1. Different.
- Both even: a,b same parity. Cases:
   * both a,b even: P_same=0, P_opp=0 (same).
   * both a,b odd: P_same=1*1 XOR1*1=0, P_opp=1*1 XOR1*1=0 (same).
   * one component both even, other both odd: P_same = 0*1 XOR0*1=0? Let's compute a1 even,b1 even; a2 odd,b2 odd. P_same = even*odd XOR even*odd =0. P_opp = even*odd XOR even*odd =0. Same.
- One odd, one even: odd has one side odd, one even; even has both same parity.
   If even both even: P_same = a1_parity*0 XOR b1_parity*0 =0. P_opp = a1_parity*a2_parity(0) XOR b1_parity*b2_parity(0)=0. Same.
   If even both odd: P_same = a1_parity*1 XOR b1_parity*1 = a1_parity XOR b1_parity =1 (since odd component one odd one even). P_opp = a1_parity*1 XOR b1_parity*1 =1. Same.
Thus P_same = P_opp always! Wait for odd+odd we got different? Let's recalc odd+odd: a1 odd,b1 even; a2 odd,b2 even. P_same = a1*b2 (odd*even=0) XOR a2*b1 (odd*even=0) =0. P_opp = a1*a2 (odd*odd=1) XOR b1*b2 (even*even=0) =1. Different. So odd+odd orientations give different cross parity. For other cases, P_same = P_opp. Let's verify odd+even with even both odd: a1 odd,b1 even; a2 odd,b2 odd. P_same = a1*b2 (odd*odd=1) XOR a2*b1 (odd*even=0) =1. P_opp = a1*a2 (odd*odd=1) XOR b1*b2 (even*odd=0) =1. Same. So only odd+odd merge allows the player to choose cross parity. For odd+even and even+even, cross parity is fixed by the component parities.

This is important. The ability to choose cross parity on OO merges gives another controllable bit. But the last choice mover already controls k. Maybe the outcome depends on the parity of the number of OO merges and the fixed cross parities.

Let's compute the total moves parity in terms of merge choices:
Total moves = (C-1) + I, I = initial_m + sum(cross_e -1).
So total parity = (C-1) XOR initial_m_parity XOR sum(cross_e_parity XOR 1) mod2.
= (C-1) XOR initial_m_parity XOR (number_of_merges mod2) XOR sum(cross_e_parity) mod2.
Number_of_merges = C-1, so (C-1) XOR (C-1) cancels! Thus total parity = initial_m_parity XOR sum(cross_e_parity) mod2.

But total parity also = product_parity(k) XOR M_parity. Therefore:
sum(cross_e_parity) mod2 = initial_m_parity XOR product_parity(k) XOR M_parity.

This is a relation. The players choose orientations on merges; cross parity is controllable only on OO merges. The final k is also determined by orientations. The game is about controlling the parity of sum(cross_e_parity) and k simultaneously. However, the winner is determined by total parity, which is initial_m_parity XOR sum(cross_parity). - If OO: the player can choose cross_parity (0 or1).
- If OE or EE: cross_parity is fixed (determined by component parities).

But wait, the total parity formula says the winner is determined by sum(cross_parity), not by k directly. The players choose cross_parity bits on OO merges. Let's compute the "last OO" game for general K,E,m using normal-play theory. Since OO merges are the only moves that reduce K, and all other moves are passes that do not affect K but may affect E and m. The player who makes the last OO wins. This is equivalent to the game of "taking tokens" where there are K tokens, and players may also make pass moves. In such games, if there is at least one pass available, the player to move can choose to pass or take a token. The outcome is determined by the parity of K and the parity of the maximum number of passes? But passes are finite and can be created.

Maybe we can find a closed form by considering the total number of moves. The total number of OO merges is K = O/2. The total number of merges is C-1. The total number of internal moves I is determined by final k. The last OO merge is the K-th OO. The parity of the move number of the K-th OO is:
move_number = (number of non-OO moves before K-th OO) + K.
Non-OO moves before K-th OO include some internal moves, OE merges, EE merges. The players can choose to postpone all non-OO moves until after the K-th OO? The ability to insert passes depends on the number of non-OO moves available before all OO merges are done. Since non-OO moves can be postponed, a player who wants to shift parity will insert one. The opponent can respond by inserting another, etc. This is like a game where the total number of passes before the K-th OO is chosen by the players, but each pass consumes a resource. The player who makes the last pass before the K-th OO determines the parity. This is exactly the same as the original game but with OO merges as terminal moves.

Let's consider the "last OO merge" game more abstractly and try to solve it. We can ignore the actual values and assume:

Let's try to compute the outcome for the "last OO" game with state (K, E, p) where p = m mod2, and assume OE/EE cross parities are fixed but maybe we can ignore them by absorbing into p? Let's define a move:
- OO: K->K-1, E->E+1, p-> chosen (0/1). Real move.
- OE: K same, E->E-1, p-> p XOR c (fixed). Pass.
- EE: K same, E->E-1, p-> p XOR c' (fixed). Pass.
- Internal: K same, E same, p-> p XOR 1. Pass.

The player who makes the last OO (K reaches 0) wins. This is a normal-play game where the terminal condition is "last OO made by current player wins", not "no moves loses". But we can convert: The game ends when K=0; the player who just made the last OO wins. In recursive terms, a player to move in state K>0 wants to make a move such that the opponent cannot make the last OO. This is the same as normal play where OO moves are the only moves that count, and passes are moves to a state where the opponent to move has the same K. We can define Win(K,E,p) = True if the player to move can force that they make the last OO. Terminal K=0: no OO left; the player to move cannot make the last OO, so Win=False.

Recurrence:
Win(K,E,p) = True if exists a legal move to a state with Win(next)=False.
Moves:
- OO if K>0: next (K-1, E+1, p') for p'=0 or1. Since player can choose p', this move is winning if there exists p' such that Win(K-1,E+1,p')=False.
- OE if E>0: next (K, E-1, p XOR c). Pass.
- EE if E>=2: next (K, E-1, p XOR c'). Pass.
- Internal if p=1: next (K, E, 0). Pass.

This is a finite DP. We can try to solve it for small K,E,p to find pattern. Note c and c' depend on component types, but maybe for the outcome only p parity matters and c/c' can be absorbed by the player's choice of p on OO? Let's assume for simplicity c=0, c'=0 (i.e., OE/EE don't change p). Then we can compute.

Terminal K=0: Win=False for all E,p.

K=1:
State (1,E,p). OO move to (0,E+1,p') with Win=False for all p'. Thus OO is always winning. So Win(1,E,p)=True. This matches O=2 always Aoki.

K=2:
OO move to (1,E+1,p') with Win(1,*,*)=True. So OO is not winning (needs next False).
OE/EE/internal passes to (2,E-1,p') or (2,E,0). Win(2,E,p) depends on passes.
Let's compute base (2,0,0): no OE/EE/internal (p=0). Only OO to (1,1,0) Win=True. No move to False => Win=False. Matches empty4.
(2,0,1): internal pass to (2,0,0) Win=False => Win=True. Matches m=1.
(2,1,0): OE pass to (2,0,0) Win=False => Win=True. Matches K2+4iso.
(2,1,1): OE to (2,0,1) Win=True; internal to (2,1,0) Win=True; OO to (1,2,p') True. No False => Win=False? Let's check: State O=4,E=1,m=1. Aoki to move. If he passes internal -> (2,1,0) which is winning for Takahashi (Win=True), so bad. If OE -> (2,0,1) winning for Takahashi. If OO -> (1,2,p') winning for Takahashi. So Aoki loses. So Win(2,1,1)=False.
(2,2,0): EE pass to (2,1,0) Win=True; OE to (2,1,0) True; OO True. No False => False. Matches O=4,E=2,m=0.
(2,2,1): internal to (2,2,0) False => Win=True.
Pattern for K=2: Win = (E + p) mod2? Let's test:
(2,0,0):0 F
(2,0,1):1 T
(2,1,0):1 T
(2,1,1):0 F
(2,2,0):0 F
(2,2,1):1 T
Yes, Win(2,E,p) = (E + p) mod2.

K=3:
OO move to (2,E+1,p') with Win(2,E+1,p') = (E+1 + p') mod2. Since player can choose p'=0 or1, they can make Win(next)=False iff there exists p' with (E+1+p') mod2=0. For any E, choose p' = E+1 mod2? If E+1 even, p'=0 gives False; if odd, p'=1 gives False. So OO move can always lead to a losing state for the opponent! Thus Win(3,E,p)=True for all E,p. Let's verify with O=6,E=0,m=0: K=3, Aoki OO to K=2,E=1,p' chosen. He can choose p' to make Win(2,1,p')=False? Win(2,1,p') = (1+p') mod2. If p'=1, Win=False. So Aoki wins. Good.
K=4:
OO move to (3,E+1,p') with Win(3,*,*)=True. So OO not winning. Win(4,E,p) depends on passes. Let's compute base (4,0,0): only OO to (3,1,0) True => Win=False.
(4,0,1): internal to (4,0,0) False => Win=True.
(4,1,0): OE to (4,0,0) False => Win=True.
(4,1,1): OE to (4,0,1) True; internal to (4,1,0) True; OO True => Win=False.
(4,2,0): OE/EE to (4,1,0) True; OO True => False.
(4,2,1): internal to (4,2,0) False => True.
Pattern for K=4: Win = (E + p) mod2? (4,0,0):0 F; (4,0,1):1 T; (4,1,0):1 T; (4,1,1):0 F; (4,2,0):0 F; (4,2,1):1 T. Yes.

It seems for K even, Win = (E + p) mod2; for K odd, Win = True. Let's test K=5: OO to K=4,E+1,p' with Win(4,E+1,p') = (E+1+p') mod2. Player can choose p' to make False, so Win=True. Thus by induction:
- If K is odd: Win=True.
- If K is even: Win = (E + p) mod2.

Thus the full game outcome for O>0 is:
Let K = O/2.
If K is odd: Aoki wins.
If K is even: Aoki wins iff (E + p) mod2 == 1, where p = m_total mod2 (parity of total missing internal edges in the initial graph).
If K=0 (O=0): no OO merges; outcome determined by fixed k as before.

Now we need compute p = m_total mod2 = (sum a_i b_i - M) mod2.
We can compute sum a_i b_i mod2 from components:
For each component:
- If n_i odd: a_i b_i even => contribution 0.
- If n_i even: contribution parity = (n_i/2 mod2) XOR q_i, where q_i = (d_i/2 mod2).
Then p = (sum contributions) XOR (M mod2).

Let's verify with examples:
- empty4: components isolated n=1 odd, sum=0, M=0 => p=0.
- P3+iso: P3 n=3 odd, iso odd, sum=0, M=2 => p=0.

- O4E0m1 graph: A n=5 odd, isolated odd, sum=0, M=5 => p=1.
- path4: n=4 even, a=b=2, n/2=2 even, q=0 => contribution0, M=3 => p=1. O=0 case.
- star: n=4 even, a=1,b=3, n/2=2 even, q=1 => contribution1, M=3 => p=0.

Now, for O>0:
- K = O//2.
- If K % 2 == 1: Aoki.
- Else: Aoki iff (E + p) % 2 == 1.
